Generalization bounds for score-based generative models: a synthetic proof
Arthur Stéphanovitch, Eddie Aamari, Clément Levrard

Abstract
We establish minimax convergence rates for score-based generative models (SGMs) under the 1 1 -Wasserstein distance. Assuming the target density p ⋆ p^{\star} lies in a nonparametric β \beta -smooth Hölder class with either compact support or subGaussian tails on ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} , we prove that neural network-based score estimators trained via denoising score matching yield generative models achieving rate n − ( β + 1 ) / ( 2 β + d ) n^{-(\beta+1)/(2\beta+d)} up to polylogarithmic factors. Our unified analysis handles arbitrary smoothness β > 0 \beta>0 , supports both deterministic and stochastic samplers, and leverages shape constraints on p ⋆ p^{\star} to induce regularity of the score. The resulting proofs are more concise, and grounded in generic stability of diffusions and standard approximation theory.



1
Overview

1.1
Generative modeling

Given data
\mathbb{X}^{(n)}:=\{X^{(1)},\ldots,X^{(n)}\}
sampled from an unknown distribution
p^{\star}(x)\mathrm{d}x
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
generative modeling
is about how to produce some fake
n+1^{\text{st}}
sample
\widehat{X}^{(n+1)}
that has approximately distribution
p^{\star}
.
To do so, a versatile idea consists in drawing an independent uninformative
seed point

\widehat{X}_{0}
from an easy-to-sample distribution
\mu_{0}(x)\mathrm{d}x
— usually uniform or Gaussian—, and then apply to it an informative algorithm
\widehat{\mathtt{A}}
built from
\mathbb{X}^{(n)}
, and designed specifically so that the pushforward distribution

\widehat{p}:=\widehat{\mathtt{A}}_{\#}\mu_{0}
of
\widehat{X}^{(n+1)}:=\widehat{\mathtt{A}}(\widehat{X}_{0})
has approximately distribution
p^{\star}
.

1.1.1
Score-based marginal time reversal(s) for diffusions

To date,
score-based generative models
(SGM) are among the most prominent generation methods.
They include
Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models
(DDPM) and
Denoising Diffusion Implicit Models
(DDIM), also called
Probability Flow ODE

(
Song et al. 2020
)
.
These methods design
\widehat{\mathtt{A}}
via a stochastic differential equation (SDE) formalism, and achieve state-of-the-art performances for all types of data except text
(
Kong et al. 2020
;
Dhariwal and Nichol 2021
;
Hoogeboom et al. 2022
)
.
In these methods, the core idea consists in designing a deterministic or stochastic forward dynamic

Throughout this article, notation

stands for a
d

Le Gall 2016

Theorem 1

(Marginal reversal(s) of an SDE,
Anderson 1982
)
.

The above result highlights the fact that reversing an Itô stochastic process
marginally
leaves lots of degrees of freedom in the
backward diffusion coefficient

b_{t}
.
For instance, the founding idea of DDIM consists in choosing
b_{t}:=0
, while DDPM takes instead
b_{t}:=\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle b\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle b\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle b\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle b\hfil$\crcr}}}_{\!\overline{T}-t}
.
Many other variants/combinations co-exist in practice, for instance with profiles
b_{t}
alternating between the two previous choices
(
Karras et al. 2022
)
.

1.1.2
Ornstein-Uhlenbeck forward, backward, and approximate backward

This work will only consider so-called
Variance-Preserving SDE
for the forward process, meaning that it corresponds to a time-changed
Ornstein-Uhlenbeck
process.
Conversely, to cover various popular sampling strategies, it leaves the backward diffusion term
b
depend freely on time
t
.

True forward

Given a
diffusion coefficient

\sigma>0
, write

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}_{\!u_{t}}
with
u_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}\alpha_{s}\mathrm{d}s
.
Therefore, all the results below extend to such a general framework as soon as
u_{t}\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{}\infty
.
In fact, statements are essentially identical, differing only by replacing
t
with
u_{t}
and
\sigma^{2}
with
\sigma^{2}/\lambda
.

Approximate backward

For unknown distribution
p^{\star}=\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern meant to approximate

, consider the surrogate SDE

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\widehat{X}_{t}=\widehat{a}_{t}(\widehat{X}_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}b_{t}\mathrm{d}B_{t}\\
\widehat{X}_{0}\sim\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern where

After running the SDE until time

, the new

At this point, note that when approximating the distribution of

•

, their respective starting distributions

2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}_{\!\infty}
and
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}_{\!\overline{T}}
do not exactly coincide, hence inducing bias.

•

For
\widehat{s}\neq s^{\star}
, their respective drifts differ, hence leading to different dynamics.

•

For
\underline{T}>0
, the early stopping make our process incomplete, hence adding another bias term.

These error terms will be made precise in Section
3.1
.
The first and third error terms can be dealt with pretty easily through the explicit expression (
1.1.2
), and do not rely on any particular training.
It is the second error term which requires an actual pre-training phase from data
\mathbb{X}^{(n)}
, which we now address.

1.1.3
Score estimation

As will be clear in Section
3.1
, a natural quantity controlling the closeness of marginals of the target process (
1
) and its approximant (
37
) is the so-called time-integrated
Fisher loss
, defined for any given candidate
s:[\underline{T},\overline{T}]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\underline{T},\overline{T}}(s):=\int_{\underline{T}}^{\overline{T}}\mathbb{E}[\|s^{\star}(t,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}_{\!t})-s(t,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}_{\!t})\|^{2}]\,\mathrm{d}t.

Despite its native expression involving target
s^{\star}(t,x)
explicitly, it can be rewritten as an average involving
s
only, with respect to an easily samplable distribution (conditionally on data).

Theorem 3

(Denoising score trick —
Vincent 2011
)
.

With notation of Section
1.1.2
, let
t\in[0,\overline{T}]
be fixed.
Let
X\sim p^{\star}(x)\mathrm{d}x
and
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d\times d})
be independent, and write
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}_{\!t}:=e^{-t}X+\sigma_{t}Z
(recall (
34
)).
Then there exists
C_{p^{\star},\sigma_{t},d}
depending only on
p^{\star}
,
\sigma_{t}
, and
d
, such that for all integrable candidate score function
s:[0,\overline{T}]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|s^{\star}(t,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}_{\!t})-s(t,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}_{\!t})\|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\|s(t,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle X\hfil$\crcr}}}_{\!t})+Z/\sigma_{t}\|^{2}]+C_{p^{\star},\sigma_{t},d}.

In the above context, data
\mathbb{X}^{(n)}=\{X^{(1)},\dots,X^{(n)}\}
allows to perform a preliminary fit of
\widehat{s}(t,x)
to estimate the true score function
s^{\star}(t,x)
.
This score estimation step is preliminary to the sampling step (
37
).
In light of Theorem
3
,
it is then natural to minimize an empirical version of the
score-matching loss
.
Hence, defining for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the loss function
\gamma_{[\underline{T},\overline{T}]}(s,x)
by

\displaystyle\gamma_{[\underline{T},\overline{T}]}(s,x):=\int_{\underline{T}}^{\overline{T}}\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d\times d})}\|s(t,e^{-t}x+\sigma_{t}Z)+Z/\sigma_{t}\|^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

(38)

the score estimator based on data
\mathbb{X}^{(n)}
is defined as a minimizer of an empirical loss function, that is

\widehat{s}\in\argmin_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\gamma_{[\underline{T},\overline{T}]}(s,X^{(i)}),

(39)

where
\mathcal{S}
is a user-defined class of candidate functions
s:[\underline{T},\overline{T}]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which will be taken as neural networks eventually.

Remark 4

(On numerical considerations)
.

Throughout, we will assume to have access to an exact integrator with respect to measure
g_{d}(\mathrm{d}z)\mathrm{d}t
, where
g_{d}=\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d\times d})
. Therefore, the values of
\gamma_{[\underline{T},\overline{T}]}(s,X^{(i)})
are fully available given evaluations of
s
, and hence
\widehat{s}
is indeed an estimator in the usual statistical sense.
However, in practice, errors due to additional discretizations of time and Gaussian Monte Carlo simulations need to be taken into account.
Such a point also applies to the optimization method used to approximate the minimum of the highly non-convex minimization problem (
39
).
All our results extend, in a quantitative way, to the case where (
39
) is solved up to small enough tolerance.
As this work focuses on the statistical aspects of SGMs, we shall hence consider an idealized framework where (
39
) is solved exactly given a function class
\mathcal{S}
.
See
Zhang and Chen 2023
for more on practical numerical considerations.

1.2
Convergence rates for SGMs

1.2.1
Prior works

In recent years, several works have established minimax convergence rates for score-based generative models (SGMs), under various distributional assumptions, evaluation metrics, and with different score estimation strategies.
Let us summarize those most relevant to our context, breaking them into SDE sampling (
b_{t}>0
in (
37
)) and ODE sampling (
b_{t}=0
in (
37
)).

•

For stochastic sampling methods:

–

Oko et al. 2023
is the first article showing that SGMs achieve near-minimax rates for density estimation.
The authors consider densities belonging to the Besov space
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{p,q}
defined on
[0,1]^{d}
. These densities are assumed to be bounded below and
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
near the boundary.
This paper works both in
\mathrm{W}_{1}
and
\mathrm{TV}
distances.
Despite a flaw in one of their core concentration technical result (Lemma C.4), its fixability (see Proposition
12
) ensures that this work remains seminal.

–

In
Zhang et al. 2024
,
the authors study subGaussian densities belonging to a Hölder space
\mathcal{H}^{\beta}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\beta\leq 2
. They show that SGMs achieve minimax rates for estimating the density in
\mathrm{TV}
distance, using a kernel method for estimating the score.
They are the first to work without any lower bound assumption on
p^{\star}
.

–

In
Dou et al. 2024
, the authors consider densities bounded below in the Hölder space
\mathcal{H}^{\beta}
on
[-1,1]
, for general
\beta>0
. They derive minimax rates for score estimation, which are then translated into minimax rates for density estimation, again using a kernel estimator. In particular, the rates are obtained for the distance
\mathrm{TV}
without a logarithmic factor.

–

In
Kwon et al. 2025
, the authors consider densities bounded below in
\mathcal{H}^{\beta}
on
[-1,1]^{d}
, for general
\beta>0
and that are factorizable.
This means that
p^{\star}(x)=\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}g_{I}(x_{I})
for a subset of variable indices
\mathcal{I}\subset 2^{\{1,...,d\}}
and functions
g_{I}:\mathbb{R}^{|I|}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.
There, SGMs provably achieve the minimax rate in TV distance and that this rate only depends on
d^{{}^{\prime}}=\max_{I\in\mathcal{I}}|I|
.

•

For the deterministic sampling methods:

–

Fukumizu et al. 2024
deals with flow matching, a slightly different framework than classical SGMs.
They provide nearly minimax rates for the flow matching version of the probability flow ODE for
\mathrm{W}_{r}
distance with
r\in[1,2]
.
They consider densities bounded below, belonging to a Besov space
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}
on
[0,1]^{d}
, that are even smoother near the boundary. Furthermore, they work under a Lipschitz-type assumption on the chosen flow, amounting to assume that
s^{\star}(t,\cdot)
is
(t^{-1})
-Lipschitz when phrased into SGMs.
Unfortunately, to date, it should be noted that a flaw — in the inner integration bounds on top of p.14 —, seems to affect the overall correctness of the proof. The recent work of
Kunkel and Trabs 2025
circumvents this issue by using velocity fields with an integrable Lipschitz norm.

–

Cai and Li 2025
studies subGaussian densities belonging to
\mathcal{H}^{\beta}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\beta\leq 2
and establishes the first minimax optimality result for Probability Flow ODE.
The authors use a kernel-based score estimator, yielding a strong estimation of the score
and
its Jacobian.
The result is almost minimax in
\mathrm{TV}
distance.

It is worth noting that numerous works on the quantitative convergence of diffusion models assume some Lipschitz regularity of the score function
(
Chen et al. 2022
;
Chen et al. 2023a
;
Wibisono et al. 2024
;
Lee et al. 2023
)
. Furthermore, this assumption appeared particularly useful for establishing convergence of ODE methods, as it is commonly employed in most related studies
(
Chen et al. 2023b
;
Benton et al. 2023
;
Huang et al. 2024
)
. In fact, approximating the Jacobian of the score is necessary when working in total variation distance
Li et al. 2024
.

The works above lay a strong foundation for understanding the theoretical performance of SGMs across different settings. In this paper, we present a synthetic proof of the minimax optimality of score-based models in the
\mathrm{W}_{1}
distance, under both stochastic and deterministic sampling schemes. Our argument builds on regularity properties of the true score established in
Stéphanovitch 2024
, which allows for a significantly more concise proof.

For completeness, we also note a series of related works that address distributions supported on manifolds
(
Tang and Yang 2024
;
Azangulov et al. 2024
;
Yakovlev and Puchkin 2025
)
. Since our goal here is to unify and simplify existing results and proofs, we concentrate on the simpler setting of distributions with densities. Nonetheless, in Section
5.2
, we discuss how our approach could potentially extend to distributions supported on lower-dimensional submanifolds.

1.2.2
Main contribution

Throughout, we will work under two complementary types of assumptions on the targeted density
p^{\star}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
d\geq 3
. Namely, a classical Hölder regularity constraint, and a shape constraint.
When combined, they ensure quantitative regularity of the score function, and hence make the overall pipeline easily analyzable from generic stability of SDEs, as well as concentration and approximation arguments (Section
3
).

Before diving into the technical details, we provide an informal version of our main results.
In what follows, the
1
-Wasserstein distance between two probability distributions
P
and
Q
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is denoted by

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{1}(P,Q):=\sup_{f\in\mathrm{Lip}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)P(\mathrm{d}x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)Q(\mathrm{d}x)\right\},

and we shall identify distributions
P(\mathrm{d}x)=p(x)\mathrm{d}x
with their Lebesgue density

p
for sake of brevity.

Informal main results
.

Given
\beta>0
, let
\mathcal{A}^{\beta}
denote the model consisting of all the subGaussian
\beta
-Hölder probability densities
p^{\star}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{+}

defined:

•

either on a compact convex set
\Omega
, with a tempered decay at the boundary;

•

or on
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with a tempered decay at infinity.

Let
\mathbb{X}^{(n)}:=\{X^{(1)},\ldots,X^{(n)}\}
be drawn i.i.d. from some unknown
p^{\star}\in\mathcal{A}^{\beta}
.

•

(Upper bound)
Let
\widehat{s}
be the estimated score obtained by score matching (
39
) over a well-chosen neural network class
\mathcal{S}
with a
\tanh
activation function.
Then for well-chosen
0<\underline{T}<\overline{T}<\infty
, the distribution
\widehat{p}_{\mathrm{SGM}}
of the early-stopped backward diffusion
\widehat{X}_{\overline{T}-\underline{T}}
from (
37
) satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{W}_{1}(p^{\star},\widehat{p}_{\mathrm{SGM}})\right]\underset{{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}}{\lesssim}n^{-\frac{\beta+1}{2\beta+d}},

(Theorem
I
)

where the expectation is taken with respect to
\mathbb{X}^{(n)}
.

•

(Lower bound)
This rate is minimax optimal over model
\mathcal{A}^{\beta}
up to
\mathrm{polylog}(n)
factors, in the sense that

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{p}_{n}}\sup_{p^{\star}\in\mathcal{A}^{\beta}}\ \mathbb{E}[\mathrm{W}_{1}(p^{\star},\widehat{p}_{n})]\gtrsim n^{-\frac{\beta+1}{2\beta+d}}.

(Theorem
II
)

1.2.3
Comments

Strength of the result

As mentioned in Section
1.2.1
,
Theorem
I
is not the first instance of a minimax analysis of SGMs.
However, our overall approach benefits from the following features, including several that offer fresh perspectives.

•

Unification.

We present a unified proof that simultaneously covers arbitrary levels of smoothness as well as both SDE and ODE sampling schemes—that is, for general
\beta>0
and potentially time-varying diffusion coefficients
b_{t}\geq 0
in (
37
).
Since the regime where
\beta>2
and
b_{t}=0
had not been previously analyzed in combination, our result also incidentally extends the existing literature.

•

Generality of the statistical model.

We deal with both compactly-supported distributions
and
distributions with unbounded support in the same framework (see Assumption
1
).
In particular, the case of full-supported densities with
\beta>2
appears to be new to the literature.

•

Proof conciseness.

A notable advantage of our result is that the regularity of the score function is not required explicitly.
Instead, this regularity is naturally induced by the regularity of
p^{\star}
itself (Theorem
8
).
This results in a notably more concise proof, mostly coming from simplifications of approximation theory arguments (Proposition
16
).

•

Methodology and neural networks.

While many prior works rely on score estimators based on smoothing kernels, we analyze SGMs trained via denoising score matching (i.e empirical risk minimization of the form (
39
)).
Moreover, it is constructed using a class of neural networks, making it more aligned with the practical implementations of SGMs.

•

Transport metric.

We derive bounds in the Wasserstein-1 distance
\mathrm{W}_{1}
, thereby capturing the geometric structure of the underlying space—an aspect not addressed by information-theoretic metrics such as the Kullback-Leibler divergence or total variation distance.

•

Weak score approximation.

Unlike for the total variation
Li et al. 2024
, we show that approximating the Jacobian of the score is not necessary when working in
\mathrm{W}_{1}
distance as a weaker one-sided Lipschitness constraint is sufficient.

•

Nearly tight rates.

In addition to the above contributions, we provide convergence rates that, unlike most prior work, are minimax optimal up to
\mathrm{polylog}(n)
factors only.

Limits of the result

To highlight potential directions for future research, let us also acknowledge a few limitations of our approach.

•

One-sided Lipschitz approximants.

The neural networks used for score approximation are constrained to be spatially one-sided Lipschitz, a condition that promotes the stability of the backward process
(\widehat{X}_{t})_{t}
. Although enforcing this constraint in practice can be challenging, several works have proposed effective methods for imposing (even stronger versions of) it
(
Ducotterd et al. 2024
;
Gouk et al. 2021
;
Fazlyab et al. 2019
;
Pauli et al. 2021
)
.

•

Time-dependency of the neural network architecture.

As in previous minimax estimators based on neural networks
(
Oko et al. 2023
;
Fukumizu et al. 2024
)
, our estimator employs different networks across time intervals to exploit the increasing regularity of the score function over time (see Section
4.1
).
In earlier works, the total number of networks used was of order
O(\log n)
.
Here, we demonstrate that by allocating more neural networks at later time steps (i.e., for larger forward times
t
), we can achieve minimax rates up to logarithmic factors, rather than merely near-optimal rates.
However, this refinement requires a total of
O(n^{\frac{2}{2\beta+d}})
neural networks.
Restricting the number of networks to
O(\log n)
would still yield almost minimax rates, following a similar proof strategy.

1.3
Organisation of the paper

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. Section
2
introduces the statistical model and details the regularity properties it induces on the score function. Section
3
presents a two-step analysis of score-based generative models based on the stability of the backward dynamic, and generalization bounds for score matching.
In Section
4
, we detail how regularity properties of the score enables to construct estimators via neural networks, and to establish precise approximation guarantees.
Section
4.3
combines the analytical and statistical ingredients to derive the minimax convergence rate.
Finally, Section
5
discusses the optimality of this rate and outlines directions for future work.

2
Statistical framework

In this section, we introduce the statistical model under consideration in the minimax result (Section
2.2
). Then, we describe the regularity properties that this model induces on the score function (Section
2.3
).
First, let us start by setting out the notations that will be used in the sequel.

2.1
General notation

Throughout,
d\geq 3
stands for the ambient dimension and
p^{\star}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
for the unknown target density.
Symbols
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and
\|\cdot\|
will denote the canonical scalar product and Euclidean norm.
The closed ball with radius
r\geq
and center
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is denoted by
\mathrm{B}(x,r)
.
The largest eigenvalue of a symmetric matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
will be denoted by

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(A):=\max_{\|v\|=1}\langle Av,v\rangle.

For
\eta\geq 0
, define

\lfloor\eta\rfloor:=\max\{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\mid k\leq\eta\}
.
Let
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
be subsets of Euclidean spaces,
and let
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{p})=:f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
belong to
\mathcal{C}^{\lfloor\eta\rfloor}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
, meaning that it is
\lfloor\eta\rfloor
-times continuously differentiable.
For all multi-index
\nu=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{d})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
|\nu|:=\nu_{1}+\cdots+\nu_{d}\leq\lfloor\eta\rfloor
, denote the corresponding partial derivative of each component
f_{j}
by

\partial^{\nu}f_{j}:=\frac{\partial^{|\nu|}f_{j}}{\partial x_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\cdots\partial x_{d}^{\nu_{d}}}.

Writing

\|f_{j}\|_{\alpha}:=\sup_{x\neq y}\frac{|f_{j}(x)-f_{j}(y)|}{\|x-y\|^{\alpha}}

,
we define

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\eta}}:=\max_{1\leq j\leq d}\Bigl\{\sum_{0\leq|\nu|\leq\lfloor\eta\rfloor}\|\partial^{\nu}f_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})}+\sum_{|\nu|=\lfloor\eta\rfloor}\|\partial^{\nu}f_{j}\|_{\eta-\lfloor\eta\rfloor}\Bigr\}.

Then, for
K>0
, define the Hölder ball of regularity
\eta
and radius
K
by

\mathcal{H}^{\eta}_{K}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}):=\left\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{\lfloor\eta\rfloor}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})\ \middle|\ \|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\eta}}\leq K\right\}.

We use
\nabla^{k}
and
\partial_{t}^{k}
to denote the
k
-th order differential operators with respect to the space variable
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the time variable
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, respectively.

For a measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we write
L^{2}(\mu)
for the set of functions
f
such that
\int f(x)^{2}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty
. Given
\kappa\geq 0
, a
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random variable
Y
will be said to be
\kappa
-subGaussian

if for all
0<\varepsilon<1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|Y\|\geq\sqrt{\kappa\log(\varepsilon^{-1})}\right)\leq\varepsilon.

(40)

Finally,
C,C_{1},C_{2},\ldots
will denote generic constants independent of
n
and
p^{\star}
, which may vary from line to line.

2.2
Model setup

Given
\beta>0
and
K\geq 1
, we now introduce the assumptions on the target data distribution
p^{\star}
.

Assumption 1
.

The probability density function
p^{\star}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
writes as

p^{\star}(x)=\exp(-u(x)+a(x))
, with
u:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
and
a:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Furthermore:

1.

a\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta}_{K}\bigl(\mathrm{Support}(p^{\star})\bigr)
and
u\in\mathcal{C}^{1+\max\{1,\lfloor\beta\rfloor\}}\bigl(\mathrm{Support}(p^{\star})\bigr)
;

2.

\nabla^{2}u\succeq K^{-1}I_{d\times d}
;

3.

\|\argmin u\|\leq K
;

4.

For all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\|x-y\|\leq(K\{1\vee|u(x)|\})^{-K}
and
k\in\{0,...,\lfloor\beta\rfloor+1\}
, we have
\|\nabla^{k}u(y)\|\leq K(1+|u(x)|^{K})

.

Informally, Assumption
1
covers
\beta
-Hölder densities that are bounded below on a given compact subset, and that exhibit a controlled decay at the boundary of their (necessarily convex) support.
Given
\beta>0
,
K\geq 1
, we define our statistical model as follows:

\mathcal{A}_{K}^{\beta}:=\big\{p^{\star}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\ \big|\ p^{\star}\text{ satisfies Assumption\penalty\ \ref{assum:1} with }\beta\text{ and }K\big\}.

(41)

Assumptions
1
.1 and
1
.2 ensure that
p^{\star}
is a deformation of a log-concave measure
\exp(-u)
by a
\beta
-smooth perturbation
a
.
These conditions ensure that
p^{\star}
, although not necessarily log-concave itself, is both subGaussian and
\beta
-Hölder.

Assumption
1
.3 guarantees that the bulk of the probability mass of
p
is concentrated near the origin, which in turn ensures that under the forward process (
1
), mass transported towards the
centered
gaussian does not need to travel long distances.

Assumption
1
.4 prevents
u
to blow up arbitrarily fast, or equivalently,
p^{\star}
to decay too abruptly towards zero.
Although somewhat unconventional, this assumption is in fact quite mild and versatile.
It is designed so as to accommodate both compactly supported and full-support distributions
p^{\star}
.
Given a compact convex subset
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, it is fulfilled for potentials of the form
u(x)=\mathrm{dist}(x,D^{c})^{-R}
with
R>0
and
\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,D^{c})
standing for the distance to the complement of
D
.
It is sufficiently mild to also encompass distributions with full support
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, such as those corresponding to any strictly convex polynomial
u
.

To showcase the versatility of Assumption
1
, we note that it accommodates distributions
p^{\star}
corresponding to perturbations of mixtures of non-degenerate Gaussians with modes lying within a prescribed ball.
In this case, the perturbation
a
can absorb the non-convex behavior of
p
inside the ball, while the convexity of
u
governs the Gaussian behavior outside.

Proposition 5

(Variations of Gaussian mixtures belong to the model)
.

Let
L\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
and
A\geq 1
. Consider independent Gaussian random variables
Y_{1},...,Y_{L}
such that for all
l\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
,

•

Y_{l}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{l},\Sigma_{l})
,

•

\mu_{l}\in\mathrm{B}(0,A)
, and

•

A^{-1}I_{d\times d}\preceq\Sigma_{l}^{-1}\preceq AI_{d\times d}
.

Denote by
q
the density of the mixture random variable
\sum_{l=1}^{L}Y_{l}\mathds{1}_{Z=l}
, where
Z
is an independent multinomial random variable of parameters
\{\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{L}\}
.

Then, there exists
C_{A}>0
such that for all function
a\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta}_{C_{A}}
,
qe^{a}
satisfies Assumption
1

with parameters
\beta
and
K_{A}
provided that
\int qe^{a}=1
.

The proof of Proposition
5
can be found in Section
A.1
.

Model properties

Under Assumption
1
, the score function

\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}_{\!\infty}=\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{d\times d})
.

Boundedness of the score

We begin by establishing a general bound on the norm of the score function.

Proposition 6

(Boundedness of the score)
.

Suppose that
p^{\star}
satisfies Assumptions
1
.1,
1
.2 and
1
.3. Then
for all
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\|s^{\star}(t,x)\|

\displaystyle\leq C(1+t^{-1})(1+\|x\|).

(A)

The proof of Proposition
6
is provided in Section
A.2
.
This estimate is sharp for small time values
t\to 0^{+}
. For instance, when
p
is compactly supported, the backward process (
36
) must transport any point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
into the support of
p
within time
t
, hence justifying the inverse dependence on
t
.
Importantly, this bound allows us to control the growth of the score as
\|x\|\to\infty
, where no accurate approximation properties of a neural network can be expected.

One-sided Lipschitzness of the score

Additionally, the largest eigenvalue of the Jacobian of the score turns out to be uniformly integrable over time.
In fact, in our context, the following strong quantitative upper bound holds true.

Proposition 7

(One-sided Lipschiztness of the score —
(
Stéphanovitch 2025
, Theorem 1)
)
.

Suppose that
p^{\star}
satisfies Assumptions
1
.1 and
1
.2.
Then for all
t>0
the score function
s^{\star}
(
35
) associated to equation (
1
) satisfies

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda_{\max}\Bigl(\nabla s^{\star}(t,x)+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}I_{d\times d}\Bigr)\leq Ce^{-2t}(1+t^{-(1-\frac{\beta\wedge 1}{2})}).

(B)

For large
t
, such a result is to be expected in the regime where
\nabla s^{\star}(t,x)\simeq\nabla s^{\star}(\infty,x)=-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}I_{d\times d}
. Proposition
7
provides an explicit non-asymptotic exponential decay uniformly over space.
As will become clear in Theorem
11
, the integrability of the right-hand side is a key property for ensuring the stability of the backward equation for arbitrary (bounded) diffusion coefficient
(b_{t})_{t}
.
Roughly speaking, it enables the use of a Grönwall-type argument to control the Wasserstein distance between the law of the backward process and that of its approximation.

Higher-order score regularity

Leveraging the controlled decay of
p^{\star}
given by Assumption
1
.4, the
\beta
-Hölder regularity of
p^{\star}
carries over and enhances the high-order regularity of the score function for
t>0
on sets of large mass.
More precisely, on high-probability subsets with respect to distribution
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}_{\!t}
, the score function is
\gamma
-Hölder continuous for all
\gamma\geq 0
, with associated norm bounds depending explicitly on time and the regularity of
p^{\star}
.

Theorem 8

(
(
Stéphanovitch 2025
, Corollary 3)
)
.

Suppose that
p^{\star}
satisfies Assumption
1
.
Then for all
t>0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1/4)
, the score function
s^{\star}
(
35
) associated to equation (
1
) satisfies the following.
There exists a convex subset
A_{t}^{\varepsilon}\subset\mathrm{B}(0,C\log(\varepsilon^{-1})^{C_{2}})
with
\int_{A_{t}^{\varepsilon}}\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}_{\!t}(z)\mathrm{d}z\geq 1-\varepsilon
such that for all
x\in A_{t}^{\varepsilon}
and
\gamma\geq 0
,

\Bigl\|s^{\star}(\cdot,x)+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}x\Bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}([t,\infty))}\leq C_{\gamma}\log(\varepsilon^{-1})^{C_{2}(1+\gamma)}e^{-t}\left(1+t^{-\big((\frac{1}{2}+\gamma-\frac{\beta}{2})\vee 0\big)}\right),

(C)

and for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}
,

\Bigl\|\partial_{t}^{k}\left(s^{\star}(t,\cdot)+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}I_{d\times d}\right)\Bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(A_{t}^{\varepsilon})}\leq C_{k,\gamma}\log(\varepsilon^{-1})^{C_{2}(1+k+\gamma)}e^{-t}\left(1+t^{-\big((\frac{1}{2}+k+\frac{\gamma-\beta}{2})\vee 0\big)}\right).

(D)

Such space-time regularity estimates on the score allow us to apply classical results from approximation theory, and to construct neural network architectures of moderate size that provably approximate it well (see Proposition
16
).
Compared to, for instance,
(
Oko et al. 2023
, Theorem 3.1, Lemma 3.6)
, this perspective significantly simplifies the study of the bias-variance trade-off in score matching, and, consequently, the minimax analysis of SGMs as a whole.

2.4
An emergent series of score regularity properties

The quantitative smoothness properties of the score
s^{\star}(t,x)
established in the previous section form the foundation for our statistical analysis of score-based generative models.
To fully leverage them in the minimax convergence analysis, it will in fact be crucial to obtain a slightly refined description of the high-probability sets
A_{t}^{\varepsilon}
on which these regularity properties hold.
Indeed, for probabilistic control, the family
(A_{t}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
must behave monotonically in
\varepsilon
, while for approximation purposes, the family
(A_{t}^{\varepsilon})_{t}
should remain sufficiently structured to permit uniform approximation of
s^{\star}(t,x)
by neural networks.

Let us introduce a compact series of desirable
properties
of the score that shall be used later on.

Properties
(\mathcal{P}_{\beta})
.

The score function
s^{\star}
satisfies properties (
A
), (
B
), (
C
) and (
D
) with parameter
\beta
. Furthermore, there exists
C_{\star}>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1/4)
, the sets
A_{t}^{\varepsilon}
in (
C
) and (
D
) satisfy:

1.

there exist convex subsets
A_{0}^{\varepsilon},A_{\infty}^{\varepsilon}\subset\mathrm{B}(0,C\log(\varepsilon^{-1})^{C_{2}})
such that
\mathbb{P}_{X\sim p^{\star}}(A_{\infty}^{\varepsilon})\geq 1-\varepsilon
and

\displaystyle A_{t}^{\varepsilon}:=\begin{cases}A_{0}^{\varepsilon}&\text{if }t\leq C_{\star}^{-1}\log(\varepsilon^{-1})^{-C_{\star}},\\
A_{\infty}^{\varepsilon}&\text{if }t>C_{\star}^{-1}\log(\varepsilon^{-1})^{-C_{\star}}.\end{cases}

2.

for all
t>0
,

A_{t}^{\varepsilon}\subset A_{t}^{\varepsilon^{2}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\inf_{x\in A_{t}^{\varepsilon},\ y\in\partial A_{t}^{\varepsilon^{2}}}\|x-y\|\geq C^{-1}\log(\varepsilon^{-1})^{-C_{2}}.

Crucially, for an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck forward process with initial distributions
p^{\star}=\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}_{\!0}
belonging to the statistical model
\mathcal{A}_{K}^{\beta}
, the score
s^{\star}
do satisfy all the properties uniformly.
This is precisely the statement of the following lemma.

Lemma 9

(
p^{\star}\in\mathcal{A}^{\beta}\Rightarrow s^{\star}\in\mathcal{P}_{\beta}
)
.

If
p^{\star}\in\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{K}
, then the score function
s^{\star}(t,x)=\nabla\log\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}_{\!t}(x)
from (
35
) satisfies Properties
(\mathcal{P}_{\beta})

with constants
C,C_{\star}
depending only on
d
,
\sigma
,
\beta
and
K
.

The proof of Lemma
9
can be found in Section
A.3
, in which the precise forms of sets
A_{0}^{\varepsilon}
,
A_{\infty}^{\varepsilon}
are given.

Most importantly, we will show in Lemma
13
that for the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck forward process, if the score function satisfies Properties
(\mathcal{P}_{\beta})
, then score-based generative models achieve minimax convergence rates for density estimation.
This fact highlights the fundamental role of these regularity properties in guaranteeing the optimal statistical performance of SGMs.

Besides the specific framework of this article (i.e. Ornstein-Uhlenbeck forward process with initial condition in
\mathcal{A}^{\beta}
), we advocate that this series of score properties constitute a base coreset naturally emerging from reasonable forward diffusion schemes starting from
\beta
-smooth distributions
p^{\star}
.
We also argue that this coreset is essential for studying SGMs overall.
Indeed:

\beta
.

As detailed in Section
2.3
, regularity bounds (
A
), (
B
), (
C
) and (
D
) naturally arise from processes
essentially
convolving a
\beta
-smooth initial distribution
p^{\star}=\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern with a gaussian of amplitude

.
These smoothness bounds make the use of standard approximation theory results.

1.

Because
